196 ΓΑΛΗΝΟΥ ΠΡΟς ΛΥΚΟΝ ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ. Ἀνεμέσητον μὲν δήπου Λύκῳ καὶ παντὶ τῷ βουληθέντι πρὸς Ἱπποκράτην γράφειν. ἔτι δὲ ἀνεμεσητότερον οἶμαι τοῖς δυναμένοις ἀπολύσασθαι τὰ κακῶς εἰρημένα πρὸς αὐτὸν ἐγκλήματα, καθάπερ ἐν δικα στηρίῳ τοῖς ἀναγνωσομένοις ἀμφοτέρων τὰ γράμματα, τὴν ἀπολογίαν ποιήσασθαι, καὶ μάλιστα ὅταν ὁ μὲν ἐγκαλῶν πρὶν μαθεῖν τὰ λεγόμενα θρασύνεται, τῷ δ’ ἀπολογουμένῳ πεπαιδεῦσθαι κατὰ τοῦ παλαιοῦ δόγματος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατὰ τοῦ παλαιοῦ δόγματος, πεπαιδεῦσθαι

196 GALEN'S BOOK AGAINST LYCUS. It is surely no cause for reproach to Lycus, or to anyone who has wished to write against Hippocrates. And it is, I think, still less a cause for reproach for those who are able to refute the charges ill brought against him to make their defence, as it were in a court of law, before those who will read the writings of both parties—and especially when the accuser grows bold before he has learned what is being said, while it falls to the one making the defence to have been educated in accordance with the ancient doctrine.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατὰ τοῦ παλαιοῦ δόγματος, πεπαιδεῦσθαι, δικα στηρίῳ

196 GALEN'S BOOK AGAINST LYCUS. It is, I suppose, no cause for indignation that Lycus, or anyone else who wishes, should write against Hippocrates. And it is, I think, still less a cause for indignation that those who are able to refute the charges wrongly brought against him should make their defence, as in a law-court, before those who will read the writings of both parties; and especially when the accuser grows bold before he has learned what is being said, while the defender has been educated in the ancient doctrine.

Greek (Kühn)

Λύκος τοίνυν ἔγραψε 197 μὲν ἐξηγητικὰ τῶν Ἱπποκράτους ἀφορισμῶν ὑπομνήματα, λωβᾶται δὲ τοῖς δόγμασι τἀνδρὸς ἑκατέρωθεν οἷς τε ψέγειν καὶ οἷς ἐπαινεῖν τινα τῶν ἐπ’ αὐτοῦ λεγομένων οἴεται καὶ δι’ ὧν ἀντιλέγειν ἐπιχειρεῖ. τά τε γὰρ ἐπαινούμενα Λύκου φαντάσματά ἐστιν, οὐχὶ Ἱπποκράτους δόγματα, δι’ ὧν τε ἀντιλέγει δῆλός ἐστι μηδὲ τὰ στοιχεῖα τῆς Ἱπποκράτους τέχνης ἐπιστάμενος. ὅτῳ δ’ οὐκ ἔστι τῶν στοιχείων ἐπιστήμη, σχολῇ γ’ ἂν οὗτος οὐ τὰς συλλαβὰς εἰδείη τῆς τέχνης ἐκείνης ἢ τῶν ἀπ’ αὐτῆς. εἰ μὲν οὖν ἐγὼ πρῶτος ἢ μόνος ἔλεγον ἐκ θερμοῦ καὶ ψυχροῦ καὶ ὑγροῦ καὶ ξηροῦ τὰ τῶν ζώων σώματα συγκεῖσθαι νομίζειν Ἱπποκράτην, τάχα ἂν ἴσως εὐλαβέστερον ἐνεκάλουν Λύκῳ πρὶν γνῶναι τὰ στοιχεῖα τῆς Ἱπποκράτους τέχνης ἐπιχειροῦντι τῶν συγγραμμάτων αὐτοῦ γράφειν ἐξηγήσεις. οὔτε γὰρ ὅσοι δόγμασιν ἐναντίοις ἐνεγράφησαν οὔθ’ ὅσοι μήτε τὰ πρῶτα γινώσκουσι τῆς τέχνης χρήσιμα εὔλογον ἐξηγεῖσθαι τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ μεμαθήκασι μὲν ἐκ τῆς παιδικῆς ἡλικίας, ἐπαινοῦσι δὲ πάλιν ἢ ψέγουσιν αὐτοῦ τὰ δόγματα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ (in σχολῇ γ’ ἂν οὗτος οὐ τὰς συλλαβὰς εἰδείη), τῶν ἀπ’ αὐτῆς, ἐνεγράφησαν, χρήσιμα, ὥσπερ μεμαθήκασι

Lycus, then, wrote 197 commentaries expounding the Aphorisms of Hippocrates, but he does violence to the man's doctrines from both sides, both in those passages in which he thinks he is censuring, and in those in which he thinks he is praising, some of the things said by him, and in those through which he attempts to contradict him. For the things he praises are phantasms of Lycus, not doctrines of Hippocrates, and in the things by which he contradicts him he is manifestly ignorant even of the elements of the Hippocratic art. And he who has no knowledge of the elements would scarcely know the syllables of that art, or of what derives from it. Now if I were the first or the only one to say that Hippocrates holds the bodies of animals to be composed of hot, cold, wet and dry, perhaps I would be more cautious in charging Lycus with undertaking to write expositions of his treatises before knowing the elements of the Hippocratic art. For it is not reasonable that either those who have been enrolled under opposing doctrines, or those who do not even know the first useful things of the art, should expound the Aphorisms; but, as though they had learned them from childhood, they praise or again censure his doctrines.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἑκατέρωθεν, σχολῇ γ’ ἂν οὗτος οὐ, ἐνεγράφησαν, χρήσιμα, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ μεμαθήκασι

Lycus, then, wrote 197 exegetical commentaries on the Aphorisms of Hippocrates, but he maltreats the man's doctrines on both sides: both through those of his statements which he thinks fit to censure and those which he thinks fit to praise, and through the arguments by which he attempts to contradict him. For the things he praises are Lycus' own fancies, not the doctrines of Hippocrates, and by the arguments with which he contradicts him he is plainly not acquainted even with the elements of the Hippocratic art. And one who has no knowledge of the elements would hardly know the syllables of that art, or of the arts derived from it. Now if I had been the first or the only one to say that Hippocrates held that the bodies of animals are composed of hot and cold and wet and dry, perhaps I would have been more cautious in charging Lycus with attempting to write commentaries on his treatises before learning the elements of the Hippocratic art. For it is not reasonable that either those who have enrolled themselves under contrary doctrines, or those who do not know even the first useful things of the art, should expound the Aphorisms; but rather, just as they have learned them from the age of childhood, so they in turn praise or censure his doctrines.

Greek (Kühn)

198 Λύκος δὲ εἰς τοσοῦτον ἄρα τῆς Ἱπποκράτους τέχνης ἀμαθής ἐστιν, ὡς ἐγὼ θεοὺς ἅπαντας ἐπόμνυμι, τὰς πρώτας τῶν εἰς τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς ὑπομνημάτων αὐτοῦ ἐξηγήσεις ἀναγνοὺς, οὐκέθ’ ὑπέμεινα τὸ λοιπὸν τοῦ βιβλίου ἀναγνῶναι, τοσοῦτον ἐφαίνετο τῆς γνώμης ἁμαρτάνειν τοῦ παλαιοῦ. τῶν ἑταίρων δέ τινος ἀξιώσαντος ἐπακοῦσαί με τῶν εἰς τόνδε τὸν ἀφορισμὸν ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ γεγραμμένων, ἐν ᾧ φησι τὰ αὐξανόμενα πλεῖστον ἔχει τὸ ἔμφυτον θερμὸν, εἶτα πολλῶν καὶ ἄλλων παρακαλεσάντων ἀπολογήσασθαι πρὸς τὰς κατηγορίας, οὕτως ἀναγκασθεὶς ἐπὶ τόνδε τὸν λόγον ἧκον. Ἵνα δὲ τοῖς μὲν ὑπ’ ἐκείνου λεγομένοις, ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ δὲ μέλλουσι πρὸς αὐτὰ ῥηθῆναι, ῥᾷον ἀκολουθήσωσιν οἱ δικάσοντες ἡμῖν, ἀναγκαῖον ἔσται μοι βραχέα προειπεῖν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὰς πρώτας τῶν εἰς τοὺς ἀφορισμοὺς ὑπομνημάτων αὐτοῦ ἐξηγήσεις

198 But Lycus, as it turns out, is so ignorant of the Hippocratic art that—I swear by all the gods—having read the first expositions of his commentaries on the Aphorisms, I could no longer bear to read the rest of the book, so far did he seem to miss the meaning of the ancient. But when one of my companions asked me to listen to what had been written by him on this aphorism, in which he says that growing things have the most innate heat, and then many others too urged me to make a defence against the accusations, I was thus compelled and came to this discourse. And so that those who are to judge between us may more easily follow both what is said by him and what is about to be said by me in reply to it, it will be necessary for me to say a few things by way of preface.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπακοῦσαί

198 Lycus, however, is so ignorant of the Hippocratic art that, I swear by all the gods, after reading the first explanations in his commentaries on the Aphorisms I could no longer bear to read the rest of the book, so far did he appear to miss the meaning of the ancient. But when one of my companions asked me to hear what he had written on this aphorism, in which he says 'growing bodies have the most innate heat', and then many others too urged me to make a defence against his accusations, being thus compelled I came to this treatise. And in order that those who are to judge between us may more easily follow both what is said by him and what is going to be said by me in reply to it, it will be necessary for me to say a few things first.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅτι μέν ἐστι θερμὸν ἐν ἡμῖν ἐναργῶς ἅπασι φαίνεται, πότερα δ’ ἐκ κινήσεως τοῦτο τῆς κατὰ τὴν καρδίαν καὶ τὰς ἀρτηρίας ἔχει τὴν γένεσιν ἢ καθάπερ αὐτὸ τὸ κινεῖσθαι 199 τῇ καρδίᾳ σύμφυτον ὑπάρχει, τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ ἡ θερμασία, διαπεφώνηται τοῖς ἰατροῖς. ἀλλ’ ὅστις γε βούλεται μαθεῖν ἐπιστημονικῶς, ἀρχὰς τοῖς ζώοις ἅπασι γενέσεως εἶναι τὸ θερμὸν καὶ τὸ ψυχρὸν καὶ τὸ ξηρὸν καὶ τὸ ὑγρὸν, ἓν ἔχει βιβλίον ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ γεγραμμένον, ἐν ᾧ τὴν Ἱπποκράτους γνώμην ἐξηγοῦμαι καὶ ἀποδεικνύω. τοῦτο μὲν δὴ τὸ βιβλίον ἐπιγέγραπται περὶ τῶν καθ’ Ἱπποκράτους στοιχείων. ἕτερα δὲ ἐφεξῆς ἐστιν αὐτοῦ τὰ περὶ κράσεων, ἐν οἷς δείκνυμι τίνα μὲν τῶν σωμάτων πλείονα μοῖραν ἐν ἑαυτοῖς ἔχει τοῦ θερμοῦ στοιχείου, τίνα δ’ ἐλάττονα. καὶ ὡς διὰ τοῦτ’ αὐτὸ τὰ μὲν θερμότερα φύσει, τὰ δὲ ψυχρότερα γέγονεν. ἅπασι γὰρ ἡμῖν ὑπάρχει τὸ θερμὸν στοιχεῖον ἔμφυ τον ἐκ πρώτης ἀρχῆς, ἣν ἐκ σπέρματός τε καὶ καταμηνίου κεκτήμεθα. διαφέρομεν δ’ ἀλλήλων ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον εἶναι θερμοί· ἀλλ’ εἰσὶ γέ τινες ὅροι τούτου τοῦ πλάτους, ὧν ἐπέκεινα τῆς κράσεως ἐξικομένης ἤτοι νοσεῖν ἡμῖν ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν ἢ διαφθείρεσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔμφυ τον (read ἔμφυτον)

That there is heat in us is evidently apparent to all; but whether this has its origin from the motion in the heart and the arteries, or whether, just as motion itself 199 is innate in the heart, in the same way the heat is too, is a matter of dispute among physicians. But whoever wishes to learn in a scientific manner that the principles of generation for all animals are the hot, the cold, the dry and the wet has one book written by me, in which I expound and demonstrate the opinion of Hippocrates. This book is entitled On the Elements according to Hippocrates. And following it there are others, those On Mixtures, in which I show which bodies have in themselves a greater share of the hot element, and which a lesser. And that for this very reason some have come to be hotter by nature, others colder. For in all of us the hot element is present innately from the first beginning, which we possess from the seed and the menstrual blood. We differ from one another in being more or less hot; but there are certain limits to this latitude, and when the mixture goes beyond them it is necessary for us either to fall ill or to perish.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔμφυ τον, πλάτους, ἐπιστημονικῶς

That there is heat in us is evident to all; but whether this has its origin from the motion of the heart and the arteries, or whether, just as motion itself 199 is connate with the heart, so in the same way is the warmth also, is a matter of disagreement among physicians. But whoever wishes to learn scientifically that the hot and the cold and the dry and the wet are the principles of generation for all animals has one book written by me, in which I expound and demonstrate the opinion of Hippocrates. This book is entitled On the Elements according to Hippocrates. Next after it come others, those On Mixtures, in which I show which bodies have a greater share of the hot element in themselves and which a lesser, and how for this very reason some have become hotter by nature and others colder. For in all of us the hot element is innate from the first beginning, which we have acquired from the semen and the menstrual blood. But we differ from one another in being more and less hot; yet there are certain limits to this range, beyond which, if the mixture passes, we must necessarily either fall ill or perish.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ γὰρ ἐπὶ πλέον ψυχθέντες ἐν νοσήματι ψυχρῷ καθιστάμεθα 200 καὶ θερμανθέντες ἔξω τοῦ κατὰ τὴν ὑγείαν πλάτους εἰς πυρετὸν ἀγόμεθα. καὶ τοίνυν καὶ τῶν πυρετῶν αὐτῶν ἔνιοι μὲν ἀκριβῶς εἰσι ξηροὶ, καθάπερ οἱ ἀκριβῶς καῦσοι, τινὲς δὲ ἐσχάτως ὑγροὶ, καθάπερ οἱ τυφώδεις ὀνομαζόμενοι. εἰ δ’ οὐ χαίρει τις τοῖς οὕτω καλουμένοις ἢ κατ’ ἄλλου πράγματος ἐπιφέρειν εἴθισται τὰς εἰρημένας προσηγορίας, ἐγὼ τούτῳ τῷ λόγῳ διηγήσομαι τοὺς ὑγροὺς πυρετοὺς ὁποίους εἶναί φημι. ἱδροῦσι μὲν οἱ κάμνοντες ἀπὸ πρώτης ἡμέρας, ἐν δ’ αὐτοῖς ἱδρῶσιν ἢ μετρίως ἢ οὐδὲν ὀνινάμενοι, τούτους ὑγροὺς ἐγὼ τοὺς πυρετοὺς ὀνομάζω, ξηροὺς δὲ ἔμπαλιν ἐκείνους καλῶ τῶν πυρετῶν, οἷς δίψα σφοδρὰ καὶ γλῶττα ξηρὰ καὶ δέρμα σκληρὸν οἷόν περ βύρσα καὶ πολὺς αὐχμὸς ἐν ἅπαντι τῷ σώματι. καὶ μὴν καὶ ἕτεροί τινές εἰσι πυρετοὶ τὴν διαπνοὴν ὅλου τοῦ σώματος ἔχοντες ἀνιαρὰν τοῖς ἁπτομένοις, ὡς νύττεσθαί τε καὶ δάκνεσθαι δοκεῖν, διχῶς καὶ τούτου γινομένου, ποτὲ μὲν ἀερώδους ξηροῦ τοῦ προσπίπτοντος ἡμῖν, ἔστι δ’ ὅτε ὑγροῦ πως φαινομένου, καθάπερ ἀτμοῦ τινος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τυφώδεις, κατ’ ἄλλου πράγματος, ἀερώδους ξηροῦ

For indeed, when we are cooled to excess we come to be in a cold disease, 200 and when we are heated beyond the latitude that belongs to health we are brought into fever. And moreover, of the fevers themselves some are precisely dry, like the exact burning fevers (kausoi), while some are extremely wet, like those called typhous (typhodeis). But if someone does not like things so named, or is accustomed to apply the aforesaid appellations to some other thing, I shall explain in this discourse what kind of fevers I say the wet fevers are. The patients sweat from the first day, yet are benefited by these sweats either moderately or not at all: these fevers I call wet; and conversely I call dry those fevers in which there is violent thirst, a dry tongue, skin hard like a hide, and much parching dryness throughout the whole body. And further there are certain other fevers in which the transpiration of the whole body is distressing to those who touch it, so that they seem to be pricked and bitten; and this too happens in two ways, sometimes what strikes us being dry and airy, and at other times appearing somehow moist, like a kind of vapour.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καῦσοι, τυφώδεις, αὐχμὸς, βύρσα, ἐν δ’ αὐτοῖς ἱδρῶσιν

For when we are cooled to excess we are placed in a cold disease, 200 and when we are heated beyond the range of health we are brought into fever. And moreover, of the fevers themselves, some are exactly dry, like the exact causi, while others are extremely wet, like those called typhodic. If anyone is not pleased with things so called, or is accustomed to apply the names just mentioned to some other thing, I shall describe in this account what sort of fevers I say the wet ones are. The patients sweat from the first day, and in those sweats they are benefited either moderately or not at all: these fevers I call wet; and, conversely, I call dry those fevers in which there is violent thirst, a dry tongue, skin hard like leather, and much parching dryness in the whole body. And indeed there are also certain other fevers which have a transpiration from the whole body that is distressing to those who touch it, so that they seem to be pricked and bitten; and this too happens in two ways, what strikes us being sometimes air-like and dry, and sometimes appearing somewhat moist, like a kind of vapour.

Greek (Kühn)

τοιαύταις ἰδέαις ἀλλήλων διαφέρουσι καὶ ﻿ 201 τῶν ὑγιαινόντων αὐτῶν αἱ κράσεις, ἃς δὴ καὶ δυσκράτους εἶναί φαμεν, ἐπανορθούμεθά τε καθ’ ὅσον οἷόν τε τὴν φυσικὴν δυσκρασίαν αὐτῶν. ἔστι γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο μόριον οὐ σμικρὸν τῆς ὑγιεινῆς τέχνης. ὅσα δὲ εὔκρατα φύσει τῶν σωμάτων ἐστὶν, οὐδὲν τοιοῦτον ἁπτομένοις ἐμφαίνει. σύμμετρα γὰρ ἀκριβῶς ἐστιν, ὡς μηδὲν ἐνδεῖν αὐτοῖς τὸ θερμὸν, οἷα γέρουσιν ἢ τοῖς ἐψυγμένοις, μήθ’ ὑπερβάλλειν, ὡς τοῖς ἀμετρότερον πονήσασιν ἢ διατρίψασιν ἐπὶ πλεῖον ἐν ἡλίῳ θερινῷ. πολὺ δὲ δὴ μᾶλλον οὐδὲ τὸ δάκνον ἢ ἀνιαρὸν ἡ τούτων ἔχει θερμασία. σαφηνείας δὲ ἕνεκα τῶν λεγομένων οὐδὲν ἂν εἴη χεῖρον ἀναμνησθῆναί σε βαλανείων, ἐν οἷς ἓν μέν ἐστι δήπου τὸ τῶν εὐκράτων εἶδος, αἱ δυσκρασίαι δὲ πολλαί. μία μὲν γὰρ δυσκρασία βαλανείου, ψυχροτέρου τῆς χρείας ὑπάρχοντος, ἑτέρα δὲ εἰ μέχρι τοῦ λυπεῖν ἤδη θερμανθείη, τρίτη δ’ ἄλλη καθ’ ἣν οὔθ’ ὡς ψυχρότερον οὔθ’ ὡς θερμότερον αἰτιώμενοι πλῆρες ἀτμοῦ φαμεν ὑπάρχειν καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο μεμφόμεθα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἰδέαις, ἐψυγμένοις

By such forms the mixtures even 201 of healthy persons themselves differ from one another—those which indeed we also say are ill-mixed—and we correct their natural ill-mixture as far as possible. For this too is no small part of the art of hygiene. But those bodies which are by nature well-mixed display nothing of this sort to those who touch them. For they are precisely well-proportioned, so that the heat is in no way deficient in them, as it is in old men or in those who have been chilled, nor excessive, as in those who have exerted themselves immoderately or spent too long in the summer sun. And much less still does the heat of these have anything biting or distressing about it. For the sake of clarity in what is being said, it would be no worse for you to recall baths, in which there is surely one kind of the well-mixed, but the ill-mixtures are many. For one ill-mixture of a bath is when it is colder than is needed; another, if it has been heated to the point of already causing distress; and a third, different one, in which, finding fault with it neither as colder nor as hotter, we say that it is full of vapour and blame it on this account.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἰδέαις, δυσκράτους, ἐμφαίνει

By such forms the mixtures 201 even of healthy people themselves differ from one another, mixtures which we also call ill-mixed, and we correct their natural dyscrasia as far as possible. For this too is no small part of the art of hygiene. But those bodies which are well-mixed by nature exhibit nothing of this kind to those who touch them. For they are exactly commensurate, so that the heat is neither deficient in them, as in old men or in those who have been chilled, nor excessive, as in those who have exerted themselves immoderately or have spent too long a time in the summer sun. Much less, indeed, does their warmth have anything biting or distressing. For the sake of clarity in what is being said, it would be no bad thing for you to recall the baths, in which there is, I suppose, one form of the well-mixed, but many dyscrasias. For one dyscrasia of a bath is when it is colder than is needed, another if it has been heated to the point of already being unpleasant, and a third, different one, in which, blaming it neither as colder nor as hotter, we say that it is full of vapour and find fault with it on this account.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁ (in ὁμοίως αὐτῷ ὁ καὶ ἀὴρ), κυλινδεῖσθαι, εὔκρατος ὢν ἐμπλήσει, δαψι λεῖ (read δαψιλεῖ)

For who does not know, if he is a human being at all, that water is often well-mixed, 202 and that air and vapour are likewise so along with it? So at any rate a stone too becomes well-mixed in the baths themselves, one on which it is possible to roll about, finding fault neither with excessive heat nor with cold, nor with dryness or an abundance of moisture. Just as, then, in water, stone and air a well-proportioned heat comes about, so too in the case of vapour, which, when it fills the bath while being well-mixed, the bathers are vexed and find fault with such a bath, yet do not call it colder or hotter; since, if indeed it does produce some such effect as well, they make a twofold accusation against the bath, declaring that it is cold and full of vapour, just as, conversely, that it is immoderately hot together with abundant vapour.

For who does not know, if he is a human being at all, that water is often well-mixed, 202 and that, similarly to it, so too are air and vapour? Thus, at any rate, a stone in the baths themselves also becomes well-mixed, on which one can roll about, finding fault with it neither for excessive warmth nor for cold, nor for parching dryness or abundance of moisture. Just as, then, in water and stone and air a commensurate heat arises, so also in the case of vapour: when, being well-mixed, it fills the bath, the bathers are annoyed and find fault with such a bath, yet they do not call it colder or hotter; so that if it also does something of that kind, they make a double charge against the bath, declaring it to be cold and full of vapour, just as also, again, hot beyond measure with abundant vapour.

Let us not, then, forget these things, nor corrupt what is known in common to all men through the superfluous wisdom which some people display; but, bearing the baths in mind, whenever we hear someone saying of an animal that the heat of this one is well-mixed, while that of this other is ill-mixed, 203 let us not transfer our thought to anything else, but to that very thing to which all men who preserve the common notions transfer it every day, in many other matters and indeed also in the baths which I have mentioned. They say, at any rate, that the bath is hot or cold, and then again full of vapour or dry and parched, indicating by each of the names some particular thing. In addition, then, to those mentioned, there is another sort of bath, full of smoke—and, yes by Zeus, of smoke of the most varied kinds. One is that from oak wood, another that from fig or olive wood, and for each tree there is a different one. But I do not yet need the particular differences. For it suffices me, for the present at least, embracing this whole genus under a single name and notion, to say that the air of the bath is smoky, here too not finding fault with its heat or coldness—or, as the case may be, finding fault with it again on both counts, if it so happens, both as hot and as smoky.

Leaving the baths now, then, recall for me what happens to us every 204 day in our houses: sometimes we find fault with the dampness of the houses in which we are, at other times with immoderate cold or heat. Do we then find fault with the air in the house in the same way in the heats under the Dog-star as when it has been filled with smoke, or is it here too, as in the baths, that sometimes they differ not at all in heat, yet one of them is just this alone, hot air, while the other is, along with this, smoky; and then the hot air never provokes tears, whereas the smoky air always does this, even if it is only moderately hot; so that here it is evidently plain that the biting heat differs not by heating more, but by something else. At any rate, standing very close to a very great smokeless flame we are often heated to such a degree as to come near to being burned, yet no one is bitten, nor do his eyes water as from smoke.

Greek (Kühn)

θαυμαστὸν οὖν εἴ τις ἐπὶ καπνοῦ καὶ φλογὸς αἰσθάνεται 205 τῆς διαφορᾶς, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἡμετέρων σωμάτων ἀναίσθητός ἐστιν ἐνίοτε μὲν ἰσχυροτάτης θερμασίας οἷον φλογὸς, ἐνίοτε δὲ ἀνιαρᾶς καὶ δακνώδους οἷόν περ καπνοῦ. θαυμάζω δὲ οὐδὲν ἧττον, εἰ μὴ συγχωρεῖ τὰς κατὰ τὸ θερμὸν ἐν τοῖς ζώοις δυσκρασίας ἀνάλογον ἔχειν ταῖς κατὰ τὰ βαλανεῖα καὶ τὰς οἰκίας· ὡς οὖν ἐν ταῖς οἰκίαις ἄλλος μὲν ἀήρ ἐστιν οἴκου καπνώδους, ἄλλος δὲ ἁπλῶς θερμοῦ, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν οἶμαι τρόπον ἕτερος τούτων ἐναργῶς ἐστὶν ἐν ᾧ πλῆθός ἐστι καιομένων λύχνων ἢ δᾳδὸς ἅμα λιγνύσι, καὶ τοίνυν καὶ καλοῦμεν, ὥσπερ τὸν ἕτερον ἀέρα καπνώδη, λιγνυώδη τοῦτο, ἐξ ὑπεροπτήσεως ὕλης γενόμενον. ἡ γάρ τοι λιγνὺς ταύτῃ τοῦ καπνοῦ διενήνοχε, καίτοι γε ἀμφοῖν περιττωμάτων ὑπαρχόντων φλογὸς, ὅτι ἡ λιγνύς ἐστι μὲν κατωπτημένης ὕλης γεώδης ἀναθυμίασις, καπνὸς δὲ οὔθ’ ὑπερωπτημένης οὔτε γεώδης, ἀλλ’ ἡμικαύστου τε καὶ συμμιγοῦς ἔξ ὑγρᾶς καὶ γεώδους οὐσίας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δᾳδὸς, ὑπεροπτήσεως, κατωπτημένης, περιττωμάτων

It is astonishing, then, if someone perceives 205 the difference in the case of smoke and flame, but in the case of our bodies is insensible of a heat that is sometimes very strong, like that of flame, and sometimes distressing and biting, like that of smoke. And I am no less astonished if he does not concede that the ill-mixtures in respect of heat in animals are analogous to those in baths and houses; as, then, in houses the air of a smoky house is one thing, and that of a simply hot house another, in the same way, I think, evidently different from these is that air in which there is a multitude of burning lamps or of torch-wood together with soot, and accordingly we also call this sooty, just as we call the other air smoky, since it arises from the over-roasting of material. For soot differs from smoke in this respect—although both are residues of flame—that soot is an earthy exhalation of material that has been roasted through, whereas smoke is neither of over-roasted material nor earthy, but of material that is half-burnt and mixed from a moist and an earthy substance.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναίσθητός, ὑπεροπτήσεως, κατωπτημένης, ὑπερωπτημένης, λιγνύσι, περιττωμάτων

It is surprising, then, if someone perceives 205 the difference in the case of smoke and flame, yet in the case of our own bodies is insensible that the warmth is sometimes most intense, like that of flame, and sometimes distressing and biting, like that of smoke. And I am no less surprised if he does not concede that the dyscrasias with respect to heat in animals are analogous to those in baths and houses. For just as in houses the air of a smoky house is one thing and that of a simply hot house another, in the same way, I think, distinct from these is clearly the air in which there is a multitude of burning lamps, or a torch together with soot; and accordingly we call this air, just as we call the other smoky, sooty, since it arises from the over-roasting of material. For soot differs from smoke in this respect, although both are residues of flame: soot is an earthy exhalation of thoroughly roasted material, while smoke is neither of over-roasted material nor earthy, but of half-burnt material, and mixed from a moist and an earthy substance.

K18a.205-206K. XVIIIA 205–206, ch. 2, 3 difficulty mid (1.69/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/7 (c) pass 13/14 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ὅστις οὖν ἐπαιδεύθη νομίμως ἐν Ἱπποκρατείοις δόγμασιν, ἔμαθεν οὗτος ὡς τῆς ὀνομαζομένης ἤδη συνήθως ἅπασι τοῖς ἰατροῖς ἀδήλου ﻿ 206 διαπνοῆς, ἣν διαπνεῖται τὰ σώματα, τὸ μὲν τοιοῦτόν ἐστιν οἷον ἀτμὸς, ἕτερον δὲ οἷον ἀὴρ ξηρὸς, ἕτερον δὲ σὺν αἰσθηταῖς ὑγρότησιν ἐκπῖπτον. λείπει τινά. Αὕτη μὲν ἡ ῥῆσίς ἐστι τῶν ὑπὸ τοῦ Λύκου γεγραμμένων εἰς κατασκευὴν τοῦ μηδὲν διαφέρειν ἕτερον ἑτέρου θερμὸν οὐκ ἀναμένουσα τὸν ἔξωθεν ἔλεγχον, ἀλλ’ ἑαυτὴν καταβάλλουσα. τί γάρ δὴ καί φησι; τὸ θερμὸν ᾗ νενόηται θερμὸν, οὐδεμίαν ἔχει παραλλαγὴν πρὸς ἕτερον θερμόν. εἰ μὲν οὖν οὐδὲν ἐν τῷ λόγῳ σημαίνει τὸ ᾗ νενόηται θερμὸν, ἐξαιρείσθω τελέως αὐτοῦ. σημαίνων δ’ ἔτι σαφῶς ἐνδείκνυται ταύτῃ μὲν μὴ διαφέρειν τὸ θερμὸν τοῦ θερμοῦ, καθ’ ἕτερον δέ τι διαφέρειν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λείπει τινά, ἐκπῖπτον

Whoever, then, has been properly educated in the Hippocratic doctrines has learned that, of what is by now customarily named by all physicians the insensible 206 transpiration, by which bodies transpire, one kind is such as vapour, another such as dry air, and another falls out together with perceptible moistures. Something is missing. This, then, is the passage from what Lycus has written toward establishing that one hot thing differs in nothing from another, a passage that does not wait for refutation from outside but overthrows itself. For what in fact does he say? ‘The hot, insofar as it is conceived as hot, has no variation with respect to another hot.’ If, then, the phrase ‘insofar as it is conceived as hot’ signifies nothing in the argument, let it be removed from it entirely. But if it does signify something, it still clearly indicates that in this respect the hot does not differ from the hot, but that in some other respect it does differ.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λείπει τινά, σημαίνων δ’ ἔτι, τὸ μὲν τοιοῦτόν

Whoever, then, has been trained in the proper manner in the Hippocratic doctrines has learned that of what is now customarily named by all physicians the imperceptible 206 transpiration, by which bodies transpire, one kind is such as vapour, another like dry air, and another issues forth together with perceptible moistures. Something is missing. This, then, is the passage from Lycus' writings, composed to establish that one heat does not differ in any way from another, a passage which does not wait for refutation from outside but overthrows itself. For what does he actually say? 'Heat, insofar as it is conceived as heat, has no variation in relation to another heat.' If, then, 'insofar as it is conceived as heat' signifies nothing in the statement, let it be removed from it entirely. But if it does signify something, it then clearly indicates that in this respect heat does not differ from heat, but that it does differ in some other respect.

K18a.206-207K. XVIIIA 206–207, ch. 3 difficulty mid (1.18/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ μὲν γὰρ οὐδαμῇ διαφέρει, περιττῶς πρόσκειται τὸ ᾗ· εἰ δὲ ἄλλῃ μέν πη διαφέρει, ταύτῃ δ’ οὐ διαφέρει, καθ’ ὃ διαφέρει, κατὰ τοῦτο 207 νοείσθωσαν αἱ διαφοραὶ. ὡς εἴ γε. καθ’ ὃ λέγεται μόνον ἕκαστον σκοποῖτο, πάντων τῶν πραγμάτων ἀναιρήσεις τὰς διαφοράς. τὸ ζῶον ᾗ νενόηται ζῶον οὐδὲν ἑτέρου διαφέρει ζώου. τὸ φυτὸν ᾗ νενόηται φυτὸν, οὐδὲν ἑτέρου διαφέρει φυτοῦ. τὸ δένδρον ᾗ νενόηται δένδρον, οὐδὲν ἑτέρου διαφέρει δένδρου. ἆρ’ οὐκ εἰσὶ τοῦ ζώου διαφοραὶ, τὸ πτηνὸν καὶ πεζὸν καὶ νηκτὸν καὶ τὸ ἔνυδρον καὶ ἐγγεῖον καὶ ἀέριον ἢ τὸ θνητὸν καὶ ἀθάνατον ἢ τὸ λογικὸν καὶ ἄλογον ἢ τὸ ἥμερον καὶ τὸ ἄγριον ἢ τὸ δειλὸν καὶ ἄλκιμον ἢ τῶν ἄλλων ὧν ἴσμεν ἕκαστον. τί γὰρ δεῖ μακρολογεῖν; οὕτω τῶν φυτῶν τὸ μὲν δένδρον, τὸ δὲ θάμνος, τὸ δὲ ἄκανθα, τὸ δὲ πόα, τὸ δὲ φρύγανον, ἢ τῶν πάντων, καθ’ ὃ νενόηται ζῶα μηδεμίαν ἐχόντων διαφορὰν, ὅμως καὶ ταύτας ἁπάσας ἃς εἴρηκε καὶ πρὸς ταύτας ἑτέρας οὐκ ὀλίγας ἀληθῶς ἄν τις εἴποι ζώου διαφοράς; οὕτως δὲ κἀπὶ τῶν φυτῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς εἴ γε., ἢ τῶν πάντων, φρύγανον

For if it differs in no way at all, the ‘insofar as’ is added superfluously; but if it differs in some other respect, while in this respect it does not differ, then let the differences be conceived in that respect 207 in which it differs. Since if one were to consider each thing only in the respect in which it is spoken of, you will abolish the differences of all things. The animal, insofar as it is conceived as animal, differs in nothing from another animal. The plant, insofar as it is conceived as plant, differs in nothing from another plant. The tree, insofar as it is conceived as tree, differs in nothing from another tree. Are there then no differences of animal: the winged and the footed and the swimming, and the aquatic and the land-dwelling and the aerial, or the mortal and the immortal, or the rational and the irrational, or the tame and the wild, or the cowardly and the brave, or each of the others that we know? For why speak at length? So too among plants one is a tree, another a shrub, another a thorn, another a herb, another brushwood; or, although all of them, in the respect in which they are conceived as animals, have no difference, would one not nonetheless truly call all these which he has mentioned, and not a few others besides these, differences of animal? And so too in the case of plants.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς εἴ γε., ἀναιρήσεις, ἄκανθα, φρύγανον, ἢ τῶν πάντων, καθ’ ὃ νενόηται ζῶα, εἴρηκε

For if it differs in no way, the 'insofar as' has been added superfluously; but if it differs in some other way, yet does not differ in this way, then let the differences 207 be conceived according to that in respect of which it differs. So that if indeed one were to consider each thing only according to what it is called, you will abolish the differences of all things. The animal, insofar as it is conceived as animal, differs in no way from another animal. The plant, insofar as it is conceived as plant, differs in no way from another plant. The tree, insofar as it is conceived as tree, differs in no way from another tree. Are there not, then, differences of animal: the winged and the footed and the swimming, and the aquatic and the terrestrial and the aerial, or the mortal and the immortal, or the rational and the irrational, or the tame and the wild, or the cowardly and the valiant, or each of the others that we know? For why need one speak at length? So too among plants, one is a tree, another a shrub, another a thorn-plant, another a herb, another an undershrub. Or, although all things, insofar as they are conceived as animals, have no difference, may one nevertheless not truly call all these that have been mentioned, and besides these not a few others, differences of animal? And so likewise in the case of plants.

Greek (Kühn)

οὐ γὰρ φυτόν γε φυτοῦ διενήνοχε τῷ τὸ μὲν εἶναι λογικὸν αὐτῶν 208 τὸ δὲ ἄλογον, οὔτε τὸ πτηνὸν ἢ πεζὸν, οὐδὲ τῶν ἄλλων τῶν εἰρημένων οὐδεμία διαφορά. καθάπερ οὐδὲ ζῶον ζώου διαφέρει τῷ τὸ μὲν εἶναι θάμνος, τὸ δὲ ἄκανθα, τὸ δὲ δένδρον, τὸ δὲ πόα, τὸ δὲ φρύγανον, τὸ δὲ κέδρος, ἀλλ’ ἐν τῷ γένει τῶν φυτῶν ἡ τοιαύτη τομή. ἀλλὰ καὶ νοσημάτων διαφορὰς αὐτὸς ὁ Λύκος γράφει παμπόλλας, καίτοι γε οὐδὲν διαφέρει νόσημα νοσήματος ᾗ νόσημά ἐστιν, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ σύμπτωμα συμπτώματος ᾗ σύμπτωμά ἐστιν, ἀλλ’ ὅμως καὶ τὰς τούτων διαφορὰς ὁ Λύκος ἐπιχειρεῖ γράφειν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τὰς τῆς πλευρίτιδος, ἑκάστου τε τῶν ἄλλων νοσημάτων. καίτοι γε πλευρῖτις πλευρίτιδος ᾗ πλευρῖτίς ἐστιν οὐδὲν διαφέρει, ἀλλ’ ὅμως ἡ μέν τις εἰς τὴν κλεῖν ἔχει περαινομένην τὴν ὀδύνην, ἡ δ’ εἰς τὸ ὑποχόνδριον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ μετὰ μὲν τοῦ κεχρῶσθαι τὸ πτύελον ἡ ἑτέρα τῶν πλευριτίδων, ἑτέρα δ’ ἐναντία τῇδε καλουμένη πρὸς ἐνίων ἄπτυστος, ἄλλαι τε πολλαὶ διαφοραὶ, καθ’ ἃς ὑπαλλάττεσθαι συμβαίνει καὶ τὴν θεραπείαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: περαινομένην, ἄπτυστος, κεχρῶσθαι

For a plant does not differ from a plant by one of them being rational 208 and the other irrational, nor by the winged or the footed, nor by any of the other differences mentioned. Just as neither does an animal differ from an animal by one being a shrub, another a thorn, another a tree, another a herb, another brushwood, another a cedar; rather, such a division belongs within the genus of plants. But Lycus himself also writes down a great many differences of diseases, although a disease differs in nothing from a disease insofar as it is a disease, just as neither does a symptom from a symptom insofar as it is a symptom; and yet Lycus undertakes to write down the differences of these too, as indeed also those of pleurisy and of each of the other diseases. And yet pleurisy differs in nothing from pleurisy insofar as it is pleurisy; but nonetheless one kind has its pain terminating at the collar-bone, another at the hypochondrium. So too one of the pleurisies goes with the sputum being coloured, while another, opposite to this one, is called by some ‘without sputum’; and there are many other differences, according to which it comes about that the treatment too is varied.

For plant does not differ from plant by one of them being rational 208 and the other irrational, nor by being winged or footed, nor does any of the other differences mentioned apply. Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλὰ ἐὰν καὶ 209 ταύτας τῶν πυρετῶν αὐτῶν τὰς διαφορὰς ὁ Λύκος ἐκδιδάσκειν πειρᾶται. καίτοι εἴτε κατὰ πλεονεξίαν θερμασίας ἡ γένεσις αὐτοῖς ἐστιν, εἴτε κατὰ τὸ δακνῶδες τῆς ποιότητος, οὐδεμία γενήσεται διαφορὰ πυρετοῦ πρὸς πυρετὸν ᾗ πυρετός ἐστιν. οὕτως οὖν ἀπαίδευτον ἐρωτᾷ λόγον ὁ Λύκος, ὥστε οὐκ αἰσθάνεται τὰς τέχνας ἁπάσας ἀναιρῶν. ἐν γάρ τοι τῇ γνώσει τῶν διαφορῶν ἑκάστου τῶν ὄντων αἱ τέχναι συνίστανται. καὶ τοῦτο ἐπὶ πλεῖστον μὲν κἀν τῷ Φιλήβῳ διῆλθεν ὁ Πλάτων εὐθὺς ἐν ἀρχῇ τοῦ συγγράμματος· ἐφύλαξε δ’ αὐτοῦ τὴν γνώμην Ἀριστοτέλης καὶ Θεόφραστος, Χρύσιππός τε καὶ Μνησίθεος καὶ οὐδεὶς ὅστις οὐ διῆλθεν ἐν τῷ περὶ τέχνης γράμματι τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον. ἔστι δ’ ἡ μὲν πρώτη ῥῆσις ὑπὲρ τῆς κατὰ τὰς τέχνας συστάσεως τοῦ Πλάτωνος εἰρημένον τοιάδε. But it is so even if 209 Lycus attempts to teach these differences of the fevers themselves. And yet whether their generation is by an excess of heat or by the biting character of the quality, there will be no difference of fever from fever insofar as it is fever. So uneducated, then, is the argument Lycus propounds that he does not perceive that he is abolishing all the arts. For it is in the knowledge of the differences of each of the things that are that the arts are constituted. And Plato went through this at greatest length in the Philebus as well, right at the beginning of the treatise; and Aristotle and Theophrastus preserved his opinion, as did Chrysippus and Mnesitheus, and there is no one who did not go through the same argument in his writing On Art. The first passage of Plato concerning the constitution of the arts is stated as follows. Socrates: ‘Sound, as it comes through the mouth, is for us one, I suppose, and again unlimited in multitude, both of all and of each.’ Protarchus: ‘What then?’ Socrates:

‘But, my friend, when you have grasped the intervals of sound, how many they are in number, as regards highness and lowness, and of what sort, and the boundaries of the intervals, and how many systems have arisen out of these, which our predecessors discerned and handed down to us their followers, to call them harmoniai; and again that in the movements of the body there are other such affections coming to be, which, when measured by numbers, he says one must name rhythms and 211 metres, and at the same time to understand that this is how one must inquire about every one and many. For when you have grasped these very things in this way, then you have become wise; and when, inquiring in this way, you have got hold of any other of the things that are, you have thus become intelligent about it.’ These things from Plato, then, would have sufficed for hearers possessed of intelligence as a demonstration of the constitution of all the arts. For it is not possible to become skilled in an art, not even in regard to a single thing, without knowing their differences; but it is no worse that, just as Plato himself was not content with the summary argument alone but added examples for the sake of clarity, grammar and music, so we too should add one further example, the art of painting, whose underlying matter is colours. For here too one may of course say that colour differs in nothing from colour insofar as it is colour, and on this ground abolish all the differences among them, which indeed Plato himself has pointed out at the beginning of the Philebus.

But, my friend, when you grasp the intervals, how many they are in number, of sound with respect to highness and lowness, and of what kind they are, and the boundaries of the intervals, and the systems that have arisen from them, which those before us discerned and handed down to us, their followers, to call harmoniai; and again other such affections arising, inherent in the movements of the body, which, measured through numbers, he says must be named rhythms and 211 metres, and at the same time one must bear in mind that thus one ought to examine concerning every one and many. But it is no worse, just as Plato himself was not content with the summary statement alone but added examples for the sake of clarity, grammar and music, that we too should add one further example: of the art of painting, and of the colours, the material underlying it. For it is surely possible here too to say that colour does not differ at all from colour insofar as it is colour, and on this ground to abolish all the differences among them, which indeed Plato himself made clear at the beginning of the Philebus.

For when Socrates was dividing the pleasures, his interlocutor would not concede that they all differ from one another 212 insofar as they are pleasures, asserting that they are most alike; but Socrates meets him with this argument: ‘For indeed colour, my good sir, will in this very respect differ in nothing from colour, by the whole of it being colour; yet we all recognise that black, besides being different from white, is in fact also most opposite to it. And so too figure with figure: in genus it is all one and the same; but of its parts, some are most opposite to one another, while others, I suppose, in fact have an infinite diversity. And we shall find many other things in this condition, so do not trust this argument which makes all the most opposite things one.’ This, then, is the passage about colours and figures.

And the passage about pleasures is this: ‘We say that the licentious man takes pleasure, and that the temperate man too takes pleasure in the very fact of being temperate; and again that the foolish man, full of foolish opinions and hopes, takes pleasure, and again 213 that the wise man too takes pleasure in the very fact of being wise; and how could anyone who says that each of these pleasures is like the other not justly appear foolish?’ And just as Plato indicated the differences of pleasures briefly in this passage, but went through them at greater length through the whole book, so too those of the arts in two books, the Sophist and the Statesman. Are there, then, many differences of sciences and of pleasures, and of colours and figures and of the pitches in sounds, and likewise too of ages and seasons and regions and diseases, and of all other things, but in hot things alone they either do not exist at all or are impossible for human beings to know? And indeed Lycus’ argument abolishes the differences of the hot, of it alone, as he thinks, but in truth, together with all the others, if indeed the force of the argument can be extended correspondingly in the same way. For in fact I shall say the following next about moist things themselves as well, asserting that these too do not differ from one another insofar as they are moist. But the very greatest of his errors is this.

That it also abolishes all the arts, 214 which have their constitution in the differences of the species. Plato, then, having first spoken in summary about the constitution of the arts, also went through the argument by way of an example, showing how the art of grammar was constituted once the difference in sounds had been recognised. But Lycus’ argument evidently abolishes this art too, and music likewise with it, and all the others, in requiring that the ‘insofar as it is conceived’ and the ‘in some respect’ have one difference in it. Observe, at any rate, how his argument will abolish first the art concerned with letters, and after this all the others as well: ‘It seems to me impossible and impracticable that sound, for one who considers this at least insofar as it is conceived, I mean in respect of the utterance itself, should have any variation with respect to another sound, varying according to some peculiarity in quality residing in the utterance.’

217 Καὶ πῶς ἐγχωρεῖ, φήσειεν ἂν ὁ Λύκος, ἐναντίον εἶναι τῷ χρώματι καθ’ ὃ νενόηται χρῶμα ὄν; καὶ ἀληθεύσει γε ναὶ μὰ τοὺς θεοὺς, ὅσον ἐπὶ χρώματος καὶ τὸ χρῶμα μηδὲν ἡγούμενος ἕτερον ἑτέρου διαφέρειν, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ τοῦτο ἀληθὲς, οὕτω κἀκεῖνο τὸ λευκὸν τοῦ μέλανος οὐ μόνον διαφέρον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοσοῦτον διαφέρον, ὡς ἐναντιώτατον εἶναι. καὶ πῶς ἐγχωρεῖ, τάχα φήσουσιν οἱ τὰ τοῦ Λύκου πρεσβεύοντες, ἅμα μὲν εἶναι ταὐτὸν, ἅμα δὲ ἐναντιώτατον εἶναι; καὶ πῶς τῷ μέλανι λευκόν; οἷς ἡμεῖς ἀποκρινούμεθά τί γε τοσοῦτον, ὡς εἴπερ οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖ τὸ λευκὸν ἐναντίον τε ἅμα καὶ ταὐτὸν εἶναι τῷ μέλανι, τὸ ἕτερον αὐτοῦ αἱρείσθωσαν. ὁ μὲν γὰρ Πλάτων οἴεται δυνατὸν εἶναι, καθάπερ γε καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης καὶ Θεόφραστος, ἕτερον ἑτέρω ταὐτὸν ὂν τῷ γένει, τοσαύτην ὅμως ἔχειν τὴν ἐν εἴδει διαφορὰν, ὡς ἐναντιώτατον ὑπάρχειν. οὗτοι δ’ εἰ μὴ νομίζουσιν ἀληθεύειν ἄμφω τὸν λόγον τὸν ἐναργέστερον ἑλέσθωσαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποκρινούμεθά τί γε τοσοῦτον, ἑτέρω

217 ‘And how is it possible,’ Lycus might say, ‘for a colour to be contrary to colour in the respect in which it is conceived as being colour?’ And he will indeed speak the truth, yes, by the gods, so far as concerns colour, in holding that colour too does not differ at all one from another; but just as this is true, so too is that other point, that white not only differs from black, but differs so much as to be its most complete contrary. ‘And how is it possible,’ those who champion Lycus’s views will perhaps say, ‘for it to be at once the same and at once most contrary? And how can white be so to black?’ To whom we shall answer only this much: that if it is not possible for white to be at once both contrary to black and the same as it, let them choose one of the two. For Plato thinks it possible, as indeed do Aristotle and Theophrastus, that one thing, while being the same as another in genus, nevertheless has so great a difference in species as to be its most complete contrary. But if these men do not think that both statements are true, let them choose the more evident one.

218 ἐναργέστερον δέ ἐστιν, ὅσῳ καὶ πᾶσιν ἀνθρώποις ὁμολογούμενον, ἐναντίον εἶναι τὸ λευκὸν τῷ μέλανι. καὶ μὴν, εἵπερ ἀληθές ἐστι τοῦτο, νομίζουσι δὲ αὐτοὶ μὴ δύνασθαι τοὺς λόγους ἀμφοτέρους ἀληθεύεσθαι, τόν τε ἐναντίον εἶναι φάσκοντα τὸ λευκὸν τῷ μέλανι καὶ τὸν αὐτὸν οὐκ ὀρθῶς ἐροῦσιν μηδὲν διαφέρειν ᾗ χρῶμά ἐστιν τὸ λευκὸν τοῦ μέλανος. ὁ μὲν οὖν ἐν τῷ τοῦ Πλάτωνος γράμματι διαλεγόμενος τῷ Σωκράτει, συγγνωστὸς ἦν ἴσως, κωλύων τὸν Σωκράτην διαιρούμενον ἡδονὴν ἡδονῆς διαφορᾷ. οὐ γὰρ ἦν που βιβλίον οὐδὲν τοιοῦτο διδάσκων, ὡς ἐγχωρεῖ πολλὰ πολλοῖς ὄντα τῷ γένει ταὐτὰ διαφέρειν ὅμως εἰς τοσοῦτον, ὡς ἐναντιώτατον ἀλλήλοις ἰδέαν τε καὶ φύσιν ἔχειν. ἐπεὶ δ’ ὑπὸ Πλάτωνος ὁ Φίληβος ἐγράφη διδάσκων τἀληθὲς, εἰ μὲν οὐδ’ ὅλως ἀνέγνω τὸ βιβλίον ὁ Λύκος, ἄγαμαι τῆς παιδείας τὸν Λύκον. εἰ δ’ ἀναγνοὺς οὐκ ἐνόησε, θαυμάζω τῆς συνέσεως. εἰ δὲ νοήσας οὐκ ἐπείσθη, μακαρίζω τῆς κρίσεως.

218 And the more evident one is that white is contrary to black, inasmuch as this is also agreed by all men. And indeed, if this is true, while they themselves think that both statements cannot be true — the one asserting that white is contrary to black, and the same one asserting that white does not differ at all from black insofar as it is colour — they will not be speaking correctly. Now the man who converses with Socrates in Plato’s writing was perhaps pardonable in preventing Socrates from dividing pleasure from pleasure by a difference; for there was not yet, I suppose, any book teaching such a thing, namely that it is possible for many things, while being the same as many others in genus, nevertheless to differ to such an extent as to have a form and nature most contrary to one another. But since the Philebus was written by Plato teaching the truth, if Lycus did not read the book at all, I admire Lycus for his education; if he read it but did not understand it, I marvel at his intelligence; and if he understood it but was not persuaded, I congratulate him on his judgement.

218 And the more evident is, inasmuch as it is agreed by all men, that white is contrary to black. And indeed, if this is true, and they themselves think that the two accounts cannot both be true, the one asserting that white is contrary to black and the other that it is the same, they will not rightly say that white does not differ at all from black insofar as it is colour. Now the man who in Plato's writing converses with Socrates was perhaps pardonable in preventing Socrates from dividing pleasure from pleasure by a difference. For there was then, I suppose, no book teaching any such thing, namely that it is possible for many things that are the same in genus as many others nevertheless to differ to such a degree as to have a form and nature most contrary to one another. But since the Philebus was written by Plato teaching the truth, if Lycus did not read the book at all, I admire Lycus for his education; if, having read it, he did not understand it, I marvel at his intelligence; and if, having understood it, he was not persuaded, I congratulate him on his judgement.

ὦ πρὸς τοῦ Διὸς ἐρωτᾷ τίς λόγον οὐδὲ τὰ 219 πρῶτα μαθὼν τῆς διαλεκτικῆς; ἔστι τίς ἂν ἄνθρωπος Ἕλλην ἰατρὸς ἢ γραμματικὸς ἢ ῥήτωρ ἢ φιλόσοφος ἢ ὅλως ὁστισοῦν ἄλλος ἀντιποιούμενος λόγων, ὡς οὐκ ἀνέγνω τὸν Πλάτωνος Φίληβον; καίτοι τί λέγω τὸν Φίληβον; οὐ γὰρ ἐν τούτῳ μόνῳ τὸν προκείμενον λόγον Πλάτων ἐδίδαξεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ δι’ ἄλλων πολλῶν ἐπεμνήσθη βιβλίων, ὧν ἐχρῆν μόνον κἂν ἕν γέ τι τὸν θαυμασιώτατον Λύκον ἀνεγνωκέναι, ἀντιποιούμενον λόγων ἐπιστημονικῶν. αἰσχρὸν γὰρ Ἱπποκράτην διελέγχειν ἐπιχειροῦντα καὶ λόγους ἐπερωτῶντα περαντικοὺς, ὡς νομίζει, μηδὲ τὰ πρῶτα φαίνεσθαι μεμαθηκότα τῶν ἐν ταῖς φιλοσόφων διατριβαῖς. ἀλλὰ νὴ Διὰ θαυμαστὸν αὐτοῦ τὸ περὶ τῶν ὑγρῶν ἐστι παράδειγμα. καὶ γὰρ τοῦτο βούλεται, μηδὲν ἀλλήλων διηφέρειν, εἰ πάντα ἐστὶν ὑγρὰ, καθ’ ὃ μέντοι τὰ μὲν ὀξέα, τὰ δὲ στρυφνὰ, τὰ δ’ ἁλμυρὰ, τὰ δὲ γλυκέα, ταύτῃ διαλλάττει, οὐκ ἀκούει δὲ ἐν τούτοις αὐτὸς ἑαυτοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διηφέρειν, ἔστι τίς ἂν

O, by Zeus, does someone propound an argument who has not learned even the 219 first elements of dialectic? Could there be any Greek man — physician or grammarian or rhetor or philosopher, or in general anyone else at all who lays claim to argument — who has not read Plato’s Philebus? And yet why do I speak of the Philebus? For it was not in this work alone that Plato taught the argument in question, but he mentioned it also in many other books, of which the most admirable Lycus ought to have read at least some one, since he lays claim to scientific arguments. For it is shameful that one who undertakes to refute Hippocrates and who propounds arguments that are, as he thinks, conclusive, should appear not to have learned even the first elements of what is taught in the schools of the philosophers. But, by Zeus, his example concerning liquids is a marvel. For this too is what he means: that they do not differ from one another at all, if all are liquids; but in the respect in which some are sour, some astringent, some salty, and some sweet, in this respect they do differ — and here he does not listen to himself.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐρωτᾷ, ἀντιποιούμενος λόγων, περαντικοὺς, διηφέρειν, στρυφνὰ

O, by Zeus, does someone put an argument by questioning who has not learned even the 219 first elements of dialectic? Is there any Greek man, physician or grammarian or rhetor or philosopher or in general anyone else whatever who lays claim to learning, who has not read Plato's Philebus? And yet why do I say the Philebus? For it is not in this alone that Plato taught the argument before us; he also recalled it in many other books, of which the most admirable Lycus ought to have read at least a single one, laying claim as he does to scientific arguments. For it is shameful for one who undertakes to refute Hippocrates and who puts arguments that are conclusive, as he thinks, to appear not to have learned even the first things of what is taught in the schools of the philosophers. But, by Zeus, his example about liquids is wonderful. For he means this too: that they do not differ from one another at all, if all are liquids, while in the respect in which some are sour, others acerbic, others salty, others sweet, in that respect they differ; and in this he does not listen to himself.

Greek (Kühn)

λέγων γὰρ ᾗ διαφέρει τὰ ὑγρὰ κατασκευάζειν οἴεται μηδεμίαν ἐν αὐτοῖς εἶναι διαφορὰν, ἀλλὰ μάτην μὲν Ἱπποκράτην διαφορὰν εἰρηκέναι, μάτην δὲ Θεόφραστον 220 ὅλην πραγματείαν γεγραφέναι περὶ χυλοῦ. καίτοι καὶ αὐτὸ τοῦτο τοὔνομα ὁ χυλὸς ὑγροῦ διαφοράν τινα ἐνδείκνυται, ἀλλ’ ὅμως καίτοι διαφορά τις οὖσα ὑγροῦ, πάλιν αὐτὴν τέμνεσθαι πέφυκεν εἰς ἑτέρας διαφοράς. οὐ μὴν θαυμαστὸν εἰς τοσοῦτον ἀπαιδευσίας ἥκοντα Λύκον, ὡς νομίζειν ἐν ὄξει στύφουσαν εἶναι ποιότητα, τοιαύτην τολμῆσαι πρὸς Ἱπποκράτην γράφειν ἀντιλογίαν. τὰ γὰρ οὕτως ἐναργῆ καὶ Διοσκορίδης ὁ Ἀναζαρβεὺς ἐγίγνωσκεν, οἵ τ’ ἄλλοι πάντες οἱ τὰ περὶ ὕλης ὑπομνήματα γράψαντες. ὀνομάζουσι γοῦν ἐν ταύταις τὰ μὲν ἁλυκὰ, τὰ δὲ ἁλμυρὰ τὰ δὲ πικρὰ, τὰ δὲ γλυκέα, τὰ δὲ αὐστηρὰ, τὰ δὲ στρυφνὰ, τὰ δὲ στύφοντα, τὰ δὲ δριμέα, τὰ δὲ ὀξέα. Λύκος δ’ ὁ θαυμασιώτατος ἤτοι οὐδ’ ὅλως γινώσκει διαφέρουσαν ὀξεῖαν ποιότητα στυφούσης ἢ πεπήρωται τὴν γεῦσιν, ὃς μηδ’ ὅλως αἰσθάνεσθαι τῶν ἐν τοῖς χυμοῖς διαφορῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἁλυκὰ, ἁλμυρὰ, ὃς μηδ’ ὅλως αἰσθάνεσθαι, ταύταις

For in saying in what respect liquids differ, he thinks he is establishing that there is no difference among them, but that Hippocrates spoke of a difference in vain, and that Theophrastus 220 in vain wrote a whole treatise on juice (chylos). And yet this very name, ‘juice’, indicates a certain difference of liquid; but nevertheless, although it is a certain difference of liquid, it is of such a nature as to be divided in turn into other differences. It is not, however, surprising that Lycus, who has reached such a degree of lack of education as to think that there is a styptic quality in vinegar, should have dared to write such a counter-argument against Hippocrates. For things so evident were known even to Dioscorides of Anazarbus, and to all the others who wrote treatises on materia medica. At any rate, in these they name some things salty, others briny, others bitter, others sweet, others harsh, others astringent, others styptic, others pungent, others sour. But the most admirable Lycus either does not know at all that the sour quality differs from the styptic, or his sense of taste is maimed, he who seems not to perceive at all the differences in flavours.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἁλυκὰ, στρυφνὰ, ἐν ταύταις, ὕλης, ὃς μηδ’ ὅλως αἰσθάνεσθαι

For by saying in what respect liquids differ, he thinks he establishes that there is no difference in them, but that Hippocrates spoke of a difference in vain, and that Theophrastus wrote in vain 220 a whole treatise on flavour. And yet this very name, 'flavour' (chylos), indicates a certain difference of liquid; but nevertheless, although it is a difference of liquid, it is by nature divided again into other differences. It is no wonder, however, that Lycus, having come to such a degree of lack of education as to think that there is an astringent quality in vinegar, should dare to write such a rebuttal against Hippocrates. For things so evident were known even to Dioscorides of Anazarbus, and to all the others who wrote treatises on materia medica. At any rate in these they name some things briny, others salty, others bitter, others sweet, others austere, others acerbic, others astringent, others pungent, others sour. But the most admirable Lycus either does not know at all that the sour quality differs from the astringent, or he has his sense of taste maimed, since he does not perceive at all the differences among flavours.

Greek (Kühn)

ἔοικεν οὖν ὡς ἀπίου καὶ μεσπίλου καὶ μήλου καὶ κυδωνίου τοῦ ὄξους αἰσθάνεσθαι, καίτοι γε πάντων ἀνθρώπων ἔνια μὲν τῶν μήλων ﻿ 221 ὀξέα καλούντων, ἔνια δὲ στύφοντα, γλυκέα δὲ ἄλλα, καθάπερ, οἶμαι, καὶ ῥοιὰς ὀξείας τε καὶ στυφούσας καὶ γλυκείας. ἐν μέν γε τοῖς οἴνοις, ᾧ ἀρετὴ κακίας ἀφώρισται, στύψις ὀξύτητος, εὐγενεῖς μὲν γὰρ οἱ στύφοντες. ὀξὺς δ’ οὐδεὶς κατὰ φύσιν, ἀλλ’ εἰ καὶ μικρὸν αὐτοῖς προσέλθῃ τοιαύτης ποιότητος, ὑποπτεύομεν αὐτίκα τὴν εἰς ὄξος αὐτῶν μεταβολήν. ἴσως οὖν ὁ Λύκος οὐδ’ ὀξυλάπαθον, οὐδ’ ὀξυαλίδα, λαπάθου τε καὶ θριδακίνης διεγίνωσκεν, οἷς στυφούσης μὲν ποιότητος οὐδὲ τοὐλάχιστον ὑπάρχει, τῆς δὲ ὀξείας δαψιλῶς μετέσχηκεν ἄμφω. θαυμαστὸν μὲν οὖν εἰ ταῦτα μὲν ἀπὸ τῆς ὀξείας ποιότητος ὠνόμασται, τὸ δὲ ὄξος αὐτὸ οὐ μετέσχηκεν αὐτῆς. ἀλλ’ ὁ μηδὲ τὰς κοινὰς αἰσθήσεις ἀποσώζων Λύκος, ἐγκαλεῖν Ἱπποκράτει τολμᾷ. πάλιν οὖν ἔγνω τὴν παρὰ τοῖς φυσικοῖς ζήτησιν ὑπὲρ τοῦ τῶν χυμῶν ἀριθμοῦ. κατὰ τοῦτο γὰρ οὐδὲν, ὡς ἔοικε, διαφέρει χυμὸς χυμοῦ, καθόσον χυμός ἐστιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔγνω, ὀξυαλίδα, ὀξυλάπαθον, ᾧ ἀρετὴ κακίας ἀφώρισται

He seems, then, to perceive vinegar as he does pear and medlar and apple and quince, although all men call some apples 221 sour, others styptic, and yet others sweet, just as, I think, they also call pomegranates sour and styptic and sweet. In wines, at any rate, that by which excellence is marked off from badness is stypticity from sourness; for the styptic wines are of noble quality, whereas none is sour by nature, but if even a little of such a quality comes upon them, we at once suspect their change into vinegar. Perhaps, then, Lycus did not even distinguish sour dock (oxylapathon) or sorrel (oxyalis) from dock and lettuce — plants in which there is not the least trace of the styptic quality, while both have a plentiful share of the sour. It would be surprising indeed if these are named after the sour quality, while vinegar itself has no share in it. But Lycus, who does not preserve even the common senses, dares to bring charges against Hippocrates. Again, then, he was acquainted with the inquiry among the natural philosophers about the number of flavours. For in this respect, as it seems, flavour does not differ from flavour, insofar as it is flavour.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς ἀπίου, ὀξυαλίδα, πάλιν οὖν ἔγνω, κατὰ τοῦτο

It seems, then, that he perceives vinegar as he does pear and medlar and apple and quince, although all men call some apples 221 sour, some astringent, and others sweet, just as, I think, they call pomegranates too sour and astringent and sweet. In wines, at any rate, in which excellence is distinguished from defect, astringency is distinguished from sourness: for the astringent wines are noble, whereas none is sour by nature, but if even a little of such a quality comes upon them, we at once suspect their change into vinegar. Perhaps, then, Lycus did not distinguish sorrel (oxylapathon) or oxalis (oxyalis) from dock and lettuce either, though in them there is not the least share of astringent quality, while both partake abundantly of the sour. It would be a wonder, then, if these have been named from the sour quality while vinegar itself does not partake of it. But Lycus, who does not preserve even the common senses, dares to accuse Hippocrates. So again he has taken cognisance of the inquiry among the natural philosophers concerning the number of flavours! For on this view, it seems, flavour does not differ from flavour at all, insofar as it is flavour.

ἡδέως ἂν οὖν ἤκουσα τίνος τῶν ὄντων εἰσὶν αἱ τοιαῦται διαφοραὶ, γλυκὺς, ὀξὺς, δριμὺς, 222 πικρὸς, ἁλμυρὸς, ἀλυκὸς, στρυφνὸς, αὐστηρὸς, στύφων, λιπαρός. εἰ μὴ γὰρ χυμῶν ἴσως ἤτοι φωνῶν ἢ χρωμάτων ἢ ὀδμῶν ἢ διὰ τῆς ἁφῆς εἰς γνῶσιν ἥκουσι. παρὰ ταύτας γὰρ οὐκ ἔχομεν ἄλλην αἴσθησιν. ἀλλ’ εἰσὶν αἱ πᾶσαι πέντε, γεῦσις καὶ ἀκοὴ καὶ ὄψις καὶ ὄσφρησίς τε καὶ ἁφή. διαγινώσκομεν δὲ ὥσπερ καὶ ταῖς ἄλλαις ἄλλα τῶν αἰσθητῶν, οὕτως τῇ γεύσει καὶ τοὺς χυμούς. ὡς δὲ ἐν τοῖς ὑγροῖς αἱ διαφοραὶ πολλαὶ, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον κἀν ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν. καίτοι γε ᾗ αἰσθήσεις εἰσὶν, οὐ διαφέρουσιν, ἀλλ’ ὁ διαλεκτικώτατος Λύκος οὐκ ἐάσει λέγειν ἡμᾶς οὔτ’ ἐν τοῖς χυμοῖς εἶναι τὰς εἰρημένας διαφορὰς, οὐδὲ ἐν ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν, ἃς ἅπαντες γιγνώσκουσιν, οὔτ’ ἐν τοῖς χρώμασι καὶ λευκὸν καὶ μέλαν καὶ φαιὸν καὶ ξανθὸν καὶ πυῤῥὸν, ἐρυθρόν τε καὶ φοινικοῦν καὶ κυανοῦν. οὔτε γὰρ χρῶμα χρώματος ᾗ χρῶμά ἐστι διαφέρει. μαίνονται τοίνυν ἅπαντες ἰατροὶ καὶ φιλόσοφοι χρωμάτων μὲν ἡγούμενοι διαφορὰς εἶναι τὰς εἰρημένας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πυῤῥὸν, φοινικοῦν, ἀλυκὸς

I should gladly have heard, then, of what among existing things such differences are — sweet, sour, pungent, 222 bitter, briny, salty, astringent, harsh, styptic, oily. For if they are not differences of flavours, they perhaps come to be known either as differences of sounds or of colours or of odours, or through touch; for besides these we have no other sense. But they are five in all: taste and hearing and sight and smell and touch. And just as we discern other sensible objects by the other senses, so we discern flavours too by taste. And as the differences among liquids are many, in the same way so are they among the senses too. And yet, insofar as they are senses, they do not differ; but the most dialectical Lycus will not allow us to say either that the differences mentioned exist in flavours, or in the senses — differences which everyone recognises — or in colours: white and black and grey and yellow and tawny, red and crimson and dark blue. For colour does not differ from colour insofar as it is colour. All physicians and philosophers are therefore mad in holding that the differences mentioned are differences of colours,

I would gladly hear, then, of which existing things such differences are: sweet, sour, pungent, 222 bitter, salty, briny, acerbic, austere, astringent, oily. But they are five in all: taste and hearing and sight and smell and touch. And just as we discern different sensibles by the different senses, so by taste we discern flavours. And as in liquids the differences are many, in the same way also in the senses. For neither does colour differ from colour insofar as it is colour. All physicians and philosophers, then, are mad, who believe that the differences mentioned are differences of colours,

χυμῶν δὲ τὸν ὀξὺν καὶ πικρὸν καὶ δριμὺν, ὅσους τ’ ἄλλους ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν εἶπον, 223 ὥσπερ καὶ τῶν αἰσθήσεων αὐτῶν. οὔτε γὰρ χρῶμα χρώματος, ᾗ χρῶμά ἐστι, διαφέρει, ὡς οὔτ’ αἴσθησις αἰσθήσεως οὔτε χυμὸς χυμοῦ, καθάπερ οὐδὲ πυρετὸς πυρετοῦ, ὅτι μηδὲ θερμὸν θερμοῦ. μαίνονται τοίνυν οἱ γράψαντες ἰατροὶ, πυρετῶν διαφορὰς πολλὰς καὶ τὸν καῦσον καὶ τὴν λειπυρίαν καὶ τὸν ἠπίαλον καὶ τὸν τυφώδη καὶ τὸν λοιμώδη καὶ τὸν ἡμιτριταῖον, ἀμφημερινόν τε καὶ τριταῖον καὶ τεταρταῖον. ἀλλὰ καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ Λύκος ἑτέρωθι γράφει πυρετῶν διαφορὰς, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τῶν ἄλλων σχεδὸν ἁπάντων πραγμάτων. and that among flavours there are the sour and the bitter and the pungent, and all the others I mentioned a little earlier, 223 just as there are also differences of the senses themselves. For colour does not differ from colour, insofar as it is colour, just as neither does sense from sense nor flavour from flavour, as neither does fever from fever, because neither does hot from hot. The physicians who have written are therefore mad, having written of many differences of fevers: the burning fever (kausos) and the leipyria and the epialos and the typhoid and the pestilential and the semi-tertian, the quotidian and the tertian and the quartan. But Lycus himself too elsewhere writes of differences of fevers, as indeed of almost all other things. What, then, came into his mind that he has now forgotten how to divide each of the other things into its proper differences, he who thinks that there is no difference even in the case of the fire kindled from chaff, such as those who work gold are accustomed to use, but that it is the same in relation to that from the other materials?

Greek (Kühn)

οὐδὲ γὰρ τὸ πῦρ τοῦ πυρὸς, ᾗ πῦρ ἐστιν, ἢ κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον, 224 ἰσχυρὸν γὰρ εἶναί τι καὶ ἀσθενὲς πῦρ ἐγχωρεῖ, καὶ τό γε τῶν ἀχύρων ἀσθενέστατον εἶναι νομίζων, ἐνταμίευτον εἶναί φησι πρὸς τὴν ἐργασίαν τοῦ χρυσοῦ. διατήκεσθαι γὰρ αὐτὸν καὶ διαῤῥεῖν ἰσχυροτέρᾳ πλησιάζοντα φλογί. ἀλλ’, ὦ βέλτιστε Λύκε, φαίη τις ἂν οἶμαι πρὸς αὐτὸν, εἰ τῶν φυσικῶν φιλοσόφων ἠκηκόεις ζητούντων ὅπως αἱ φλόγες γεννῶνται κατανενόηκας ἂν οἶμαι τὰς ἐν αὐταῖς διαφοράς. ἐγὼ τοίνυν ὅσον οἷόν τε διὰ βραχυτάτων ἐπιτέμνεσθαι λόγον οὐ μικρὸν ἐξηγήσομαί σοι περὶ αὐτῶν. ὁρᾶς, οἶμαι, τῆς καιομένης ὕλης ἀναθυμίασίν τινα γιγνομένην ἐν διεσπασμένοις ἀπ’ ἀλλήλων μορίοις, ὧν μεταξύ τις ἀὴρ ἀπολαμβανόμενος, ἐξ ἀνάγκης μὲν δήπου θερμαίνεται. παμπόλλην δὲ ἴσχει διαφορὰν ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐνταμίευτον, ἢ κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον

For neither does fire differ from fire, insofar as it is fire, except in respect of the more and the less — 224 for it is possible for one fire to be strong and another weak — and, thinking that the fire of chaff is the weakest, he says that it is serviceable for the working of gold; for the gold, he says, melts and flows away when it comes near a stronger flame. But, my excellent Lycus, someone might say to him, I think, if you had heard the natural philosophers inquiring how flames are generated, you would, I think, have understood the differences among them. I, then, cutting short as far as possible, in the briefest terms, an argument that is no small one, shall explain them to you. You see, I think, that a certain exhalation arises from the burning material in particles torn apart from one another, between which a certain air is caught, which of necessity, surely, is heated; but it admits of a very great difference in respect of the more and the less.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἢ κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλον, ἐνταμίευτον, διαῤῥεῖν, ἐπιτέμνεσθαι

For neither does fire differ from fire, insofar as it is fire, except by the more and the less 224 (for it is possible for fire to be strong and weak); and thinking that the fire of chaff is the weakest, he says it is reserved for the working of gold. For the gold melts away and runs off when it comes near a stronger flame. But, my excellent Lycus, someone might say to him, I think, if you had listened to the natural philosophers inquiring how flames are generated, you would, I think, have perceived the differences in them. I, then, condensing as far as possible into the briefest compass an account of no small length, will explain to you about them. You see, I think, that from the burning material an exhalation arises in parts drawn apart from one another, and some air, enclosed between them, is of necessity, surely, heated. And it admits of a very great difference in the more and the less.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ διαλείμματα τῶν ἀναφερομένων σωμάτων οὐκ ἴσα πάσαις ταῖς φλοξίν ἐστιν, ὥσπερ οὐδ’ αὐτῆς τῆς ἀναθυμιάσεως ἡ φύσις, ἀλλὰ καὶ μεγέθει καὶ σμικρότητι διενήνοχεν 225 ἀλλήλων τὰ τῆς καιομένης ὕλης ἀναθέοντα μόρια καὶ πυκνότητι καὶ μανότητι καὶ τῷ τὰ μὲν ἡμίκαυτα, τὰ δ’ οἷον ὁλόκαυτα ὑπάρχειν, ἢ ὅλως τὰ μὲν μᾶλλον, τὰ δὲ ἧττον ἐκπυροῦσθαι. ὅταν οὖν ταῦτα φαίνηται τοσαύτην ἔχοντα τὴν διαφορὰν, ὅ τε ἀὴρ ὁ δεδεγμένος αὐτὰ ποτὲ μὲν μᾶλλον ὑπ’ αὐτῶν ἐκτεθερμασμένος ᾖ, ποτὲ δ’ ἧττον, ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι παμπόλλην γε ποικιλίαν γίγνεσθαι καὶ διαφορὰν οὐκ ὀλίγην τῆς φλογός. ἡ γὰρ τῆς μίξεως ἀνομοιότης τὸ πλῆθος τῶν διαφορῶν ἐργάζεται. Ὥσπερ δὲ τὰ περὶ τοῦ πυρὸς ἀμαθῶς εἴρηται τῷ Λύκῳ, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν, οἶμαι, τρόπον κἀπειδὰν φάμενος τριχῶς ἐννοεῖσθαι τὸ πολὺ θερμὸν, ἕκαστον τῶν σημαινομένων ἐπεξέρχεται. τὸ μὲν γάρ τι κατὰ τὴν οὐσίαν φησὶν ἐννοεῖσθαι τὸ πολὺ θερμὸν, τὸ δὲ τι κατὰ τὴν ἐπίστασιν τῆς ποιότητος, τὸ δέ τι κατὰ τὴν ἰσχὺν τῆς οἰκείας ἐνεργείας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπίστασιν, κἀπειδὰν φάμενος

But further, the intervals between the bodies carried upward are not equal in all flames, just as neither is the nature of the exhalation itself; rather, the particles that run up from the burning material differ 225 from one another both in largeness and in smallness, and in density and in rarity, and in that some are half-burnt while others are, as it were, wholly burnt, or, in general, some are more and others less set ablaze. When, therefore, these are seen to have so great a difference, and the air that has received them is at one time more heated by them and at another time less, it is necessary that a very great variety should arise and no small difference in the flame; for the dissimilarity of the mixture produces the multitude of the differences. And just as what concerns fire has been said ignorantly by Lycus, in the same way, I think, also when, having stated that ‘much heat’ is understood in three ways, he goes through each of the senses. For he says that ‘much heat’ is understood in one way in respect of substance, in another in respect of the intensification of the quality, and in another in respect of the strength of its proper activity.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναθέοντα, ἐκπυροῦσθαι, ἐπίστασιν

But also the intervals between the bodies carried upward are not equal in all flames, just as neither is the nature of the exhalation itself; rather, the parts of the burning material that rush upward have differed from 225 one another in largeness and smallness, and in density and rarity, and in that some are half-burnt, others as it were wholly burnt, or in general that some are more, others less, converted into fire. When, then, these appear to have so great a difference, and the air that has received them is at one time more thoroughly heated by them, at another less, it is necessary that a very great variety arise, and no small difference of the flame. For the unlikeness of the mixture produces the multitude of differences. And just as what concerns fire has been stated ignorantly by Lycus, in the same way, I think, also when, saying that 'much hot' is conceived in three ways, he goes through each of the meanings. For he says that 'much hot' is conceived in one sense according to substance, in another according to the intensity of the quality, and in another according to the strength of its proper activity.

﻿ 226 ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ κατὰ τὴν οὐσίαν εἶναι τοῖς αὐξανομένοις πολὺ θερμὸν ἐπὶ σμι κρῷ γε τῷ τοῦ σώματος ὄγκῳ οὔτε κατὰ τὴν τῆς ποιότητος αὔξησιν, οὐ γὰρ φαίνεται θερμότερον τῶν ἀκμαζόντων. ἐν μὲν δὴ τῇ τῶν οἰκείων ἔργων ἰσχύϊ συγχωρεῖ μὲν ὑπερέχειν τὸ τῶν αὐξανομένων θερμὸν, ἀλλ’ οὐ κυρίως λέγεσθαι. ἔστι μὲν οὖν ἡ ῥῆσις αὐτοῦ, δι’ ἧς ταῦτα δηλώσει, μεγίστη. ἀλλ’ ἐγώ μοι δοκῶ, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῆς προγεγραμμένης ἔπραξα, καὶ νῦν οὕτω ποιήσειν. οὖσαν γὰρ κἀκείνην μακροτάτην οὐχ ὅλην ἔγραψα, μόνον δὲ τὸ ἐπικαιρότατον αὐτῆς ἐξέλεξα. γιγνέσθω τοίνυν κἀπὶ ταύτης οὕτως καὶ γραφέσθω τὸ χρησιμώτατον εἰς τὸ προκείμενον μέρος αὐτῆς. ὁ μὲν ἕτερος οὖν μοι τῶν λόγων καὶ δὴ πέρας ἔχει, ἐπὶ δὲ τὸν λοιπὸν ἂν ἀφικοίμην, δύο γὰρ δήπου μεμνῆσθαί μου προθεμένας εὐθὺς ἐπιζητήσεις ἐν ἀρχῇ, μίαν μὲν τὴν εἰ ὀρθῶς προσέθηκεν Ἱπποκράτης τὸ ἔμφυτον, ἄλλην δὲ εἰ ὀρθῶς εἶπε, πλεῖστον εἶναι τοῖς αὐξανομένοις τὸ θερμόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σμι κρῷ, μεμνῆσθαί μου προθεμένας, κυρίως λέγεσθαι

226 But [he says] that in those who are growing the heat is not great according to substance either, given at any rate that the bulk of the body is small, nor according to the increase of the quality, for it does not appear hotter than that of those in their prime. In the strength of its proper works, indeed, he concedes that the heat of those who are growing is superior, but [says] that this is not what is properly meant by the word. Now his passage, through which he will make these things clear, is very long. Let it be done, then, in the same way with this one too, and let the part of it most useful for the matter in hand be written out. "The one of my two discussions, then, has indeed reached its end, and I may now come to the remaining one; for surely [one must] remember that I set out two inquiries right at the beginning: one, whether Hippocrates was right to add 'innate', the other, whether he was right to say that the heat is greatest in those who are growing.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σμι κρῷ, κυρίως, δηλώσει

226 But [he says] that in growing bodies the heat is not much in respect of substance, at least given the small bulk of their body, nor in respect of the increase of the quality, for it does not appear hotter than that of those in their prime. In the strength of its proper works, then, he concedes that the heat of growing bodies excels, but [holds] that it is not said in the proper sense. Now his passage, in which he will make these points clear, is very long. But I think I shall do now just as I did with the passage written out earlier. τῆς δ’ ἑτέρας οὖν ζητήσεως 227 ἐκτετελεσμένης, ἐπὶ ταύτην οὖν λοιπὸν τραποίμην καὶ ἐπιχειρήσαιμι ἀνασκοπεῖσθαι, εἰ ὀρθῶς λέλεκται, πλεῖστον εἶναι τοῖς αὐξανομένοις τὸ θερμόν. κατὰ δὴ τὸ ἐξετάζειν ἕτερον θερμὸν, τριττὰ τυγχάνει ὄντα τὰ ἐπιζητήσεως δεόμενα καὶ μάλα πολὺ κεχωρισμένα ἀλλήλων. ἓν μὲν τί ποτέ ἐστι τὸ πλεῖον ἄλλο ἄλλου θερμὸν, ἕτερον δὲ τί ποτέ ἐστιν ἄλλο ἄλλου θερμότερον, καὶ τρίτον τί ποτέ ἐστιν ἄλλο ἄλλου θερμὸν ἰσχυρότερον. εἰ μὲν οὖν τύχῃ τις ἐρόμενος ῾μᾶς ποῖόν τί ἐστι τὸ πλεῖον ἄλλο ἄλλου θερμὸν, τὸ ἀνάλογον ὀφείλομεν ἀποκρίνεσθαι ὡς καὶ πρὸς τὸν ἕτερον ἐρόμενον ποῖόν τί ἐστιν ἕτερον ἑτέρου ὑγρὸν πλεῖον ἀποκρινοίμεθα. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἐνταῦθα πρῶτον τὸ ἐπερώτημα πρὸς τὸ εἰρημένον ἐστὶν, οὐκέτι ἄλλο τι ὀρθῶς ἀποκρίνεσθαι ἀλλ’ ἢ μόνον τοῦτο τὸ τῇ οὐσίᾳ ὑπερβάλλειν πλεῖον, ἀμέλει ὑγρὸν ἄν τις εἴποι τὸν ἀμφορέα τοῦ ἡμίσεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῾μᾶς, ἐνταῦθα πρῶτον τὸ ἐπερώτημα πρὸς τὸ εἰρημένον, ἀμέλει, ἡμίσεως

Now that the first inquiry 227 has been brought to completion, I may turn next to this one and attempt to examine whether it has been rightly said that the heat is greatest in those who are growing. In examining one heat against another, the things that require inquiry happen to be three, and very widely separated from one another: one, what it is for one thing to be more heat than another; a second, what it is for one thing to be hotter than another; and a third, what it is for one thing to be a stronger heat than another. If, then, someone should happen to ask us what it is for one thing to be more heat than another, we ought to give the answer analogous to the one we would give to the other man who asks what it is for one thing to be more moisture than another. For just as there, once the question is put in the first place with reference to what has been stated, it is no longer correct to answer anything else except this alone, that it exceeds in substance, being more: one would doubtless say that the amphora is more moisture than the half-amphora.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῾μᾶς, πρῶτον τὸ ἐπερώτημα πρὸς τὸ εἰρημένον, ἀμέλει, ἡμίσεως

The other inquiry, then, having been 227 completed, I would now turn to this remaining one and attempt to examine whether it has been rightly said that heat is greatest in growing bodies. Now in examining one heat against another, there happen to be three things that require inquiry, and these very far separated from one another: one, what it is for one heat to be more than another; another, what it is for one thing to be hotter than another; and the third, what it is for one heat to be stronger than another. If, then, someone should happen to ask us of what sort is the heat that is more than another, we ought to answer analogously to what we would answer to another who asked of what sort is one moist thing that is more than another. For just as here, the question being directed in the first place to what was said, it is no longer correct to answer anything else than only this, that what exceeds in substance is more: one would, of course, say that the amphora [holds] more liquid than the half.

So too in the case of heat: if someone were to put this same question, what it is for one thing to be more heat than the other, we should not correctly 228 answer anything other than that which is more in substance, evidently because it is more in substance and extends over more place. And nothing at all will prevent the same thing from being both more heat and heat to a lesser degree, just as the thing that is more moisture in substance could also be less diffused. To this question, then, one would have answered correctly in this way; but to that other question, what it is for one thing to be hotter than another, the correct answer is that which is more intensified in respect of warming, even if it happens to be in a smaller substance. Just as to one asking what it is for one thing to be whiter than another it is correct to answer: that which is more intensified in respect of the quality white. In this respect, at any rate, even if snow happens to be present in a small bulk, one would say it is whiter than any other white thing whatsoever; just as also to one asking what it is for one thing to be moister than another it is correct to answer: that which is more diffused, as water is than honey and pitch, even if the water is observed in a very small substance while the honey and the pitch are in excess.

So too in the case of heat: if someone were to put this same question, of what sort is the heat that is more than another, we would not rightly 228 answer anything other than that which is more in substance, because, plainly, it has more substance and extends over a larger place. So too 229 to one asking about heat what it is for one thing to be hotter than another it is correct to answer: that which is most intensified in respect of warming, as for instance fire, even if it is observed in a small amount of substance, while some other heat, one that is in fact lukewarm, when compared against it happens to exist in a greater substance. To these questions, then, these are the answers; but to one who inquires what it is for one thing to be a stronger heat than another, the answer is no longer like the previous ones. For we shall answer neither that which is more intensified in respect of warming nor that which is observed in a greater substance, but that which excels in the accomplishment of its work. What I mean would be established as follows. The works of heat are, surely, many. For some things are melted by it, and it makes others set, and it transforms very many things; and, to speak of our bodies in particular, it digests the food in the stomach and the intestines, and conveys the product of its working-up into the body, and alters it into body and attaches it to our bodies, in nutrition and in growth.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐκ ἂν ἐπ’ ὀλίγης, ἀντεξεταζόμενον, χεῖται, κατεργασίαν, προσφύει

So also, 229 to one asking about heat of what sort is one thing hotter than another, it is correct to answer: that which is most intensified in warmth, as fire, even though it is observed in a small substance, [is hotter than] some other hot and lukewarm thing that is compared with it and happens to exist in a larger substance. To these questions, then, these are the answers; but to one who inquires of what sort is one heat stronger than another, the answer is no longer like the previous ones. For we shall answer neither that which is more intensified in warmth nor that which is observed in a larger substance, but that which excels in the accomplishment of the work. What I am saying would be established thus: the works of heat are, I suppose, many. For some things are melted by it, and others it solidifies, and it changes a great many things; and, to speak in detail, in our bodies it concocts the nutriment in the stomach and the intestines, and brings the worked-up [nutriment] up into the body, and alters it into body and makes it grow onto our bodies, namely in nutrition and growth.

One heat, then, is stronger than another when it is the one that will be able to furnish 230 these things to a greater degree. And what happens to be such is not in every case the one that is either intensified in respect of quality or greater in substance. For often that which is less in substance and less in warming is the most effective for the work. For, as we said before, with respect to the performance of its proper works it is not the most intensified heat that is stronger, but the most well-proportioned; since that which has been intensified in respect of warming itself sometimes even becomes a harm to the works, and for this I have as most trustworthy and sufficient [evidence] the things I set out before, when speaking about the peculiar nature of heat.

For, as we said before, for the performance of the proper works it is not what is most intensified that is stronger, but what is most well-proportioned. For what is intensified in warmth itself sometimes even becomes a harm to the works; and sufficient and most credible proofs of this, to my mind, are the things I went through earlier when speaking about the distinctive property of heat.
